[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.22, F32.0, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I’m here because I’ve been feeling more wound up than usual, and it’s starting to show in places I don’t want it to. My father-in-law moved in with us, and since then I’ve been kind of holding it together at work — I’m an EMT, so you sort of have to keep things contained there — but when I get home, or when I try to sit down for my coursework, I’m short, tense, just… not myself.\n\nThe thing that made me think maybe I should talk to someone is that I’ve started dropping the things that usually keep me level. I fish, I do woodworking, and I used to go to first-aid workshops, partly because I enjoy them and partly because they clear my head. Lately I’ve been skipping them. I tell myself I’m too busy or too tired, but I think the truth is I’m just stressed and not handling the change at home as well as I expected to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a number of different things, like work, family, or health, and does it feel hard to control the worry?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “a number of different things,” exactly. I mean, I do think about work, and family, and whether I’m doing enough, sure — but it doesn’t feel like I’m lying awake making a whole list of disasters, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more like… there’s this one main pressure point at home, and then everything else kind of gets filtered through that. So if something small happens, I react like I’m already at capacity. But I’m not sure it’s worry in the sense of, like, constant uncontrollable worrying about my health or money or every possible thing. It’s more tension, I guess, and having a hard time switching it off once I’m irritated or keyed up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical changes when you're feeling this tension, like trouble sleeping, shaking, sweating, or muscle tightness?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure I’ve been tracking it closely enough to give you a clean answer. I mean, when I’m tense, I do feel it in my body in the general sense — like I’m aware I’m keyed up — but I couldn’t honestly say, “yes, I’m shaking” or “yes, I’m sweating,” because I haven’t really noticed those specifically.\n\nSleep… I guess I’d have to think about that more. I don’t want to overstate it. I have nights where I don’t feel settled right away, but I’m not sure if that’s anything unusual or just me having a lot on my mind after the day. And muscle tightness — maybe? I mean, I’m an EMT, so I’m used to being physically tense during shifts, lifting, driving, being alert, all that. Sometimes it’s hard to separate what’s stress and what’s just the job or getting older.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: I haven’t noticed a big obvious physical thing like shaking or sweating. It’s more that I feel wound up, like my system doesn’t downshift as easily, but I haven’t paid enough attention to say much beyond that.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the situation with your father-in-law, do you ever feel restless or have trouble relaxing when there isn’t an obvious reason for it?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. I mean, I’m not saying I’m always perfectly relaxed when nothing’s going on — I’m an EMT, so being a little keyed up can kind of come with the territory — but what brought me in is that this feels tied pretty clearly to the change at home.\n\nSince my father-in-law moved in, it’s like I can keep the lid on during work because I have to, but then when I get home, or when I’m trying to sit down and focus on coursework, I’m already wound up. I get short faster than I normally would, and I have a harder time settling myself.\n\nAnd the thing that makes me think it’s not just random restlessness is that I’ve started dropping the stuff that usually helps me relax — fishing, woodworking, even those first-aid workshops I used to like going to. I’ll skip them and tell myself I’m busy or tired, but really I think I’m just not dealing with this home situation as well as I thought I was. So outside of that, no, I don’t think I’m just restless for no reason. It feels more connected to all of this.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even outside of the stress at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I feel hopeless exactly. That word feels a little bigger than what I’m experiencing. But I have noticed I’m not doing the things that normally help me feel like myself.\n\nLike, since my father-in-law moved in with us, I’ve been able to keep it together at work because I kind of have to — you can’t be distracted on calls, you know — but then when I get home, or when I’m trying to sit down and work on coursework, I’m already wound up. I’m more short with people than I’m used to being, and I don’t like that about myself.\n\nAnd the interest thing… yeah, that part’s probably fair. I’ve been skipping fishing, woodworking, even the first-aid workshops I used to enjoy going to. I’ll tell myself I’m just busy or tired, but if I’m being honest, those are usually the things that keep my head clear, and lately I just haven’t been making myself do them. So I don’t know if I’d call it being “down” across the board, but I’m definitely not getting the same relief from my usual routines because I’m not really doing them.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or in a low mood for most of the day, and if so, how often has that happened over the last couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve had days where I was down for most of the day. That doesn’t quite feel accurate to me.\n\nI’ve had stretches where I feel worn out or kind of discouraged, especially if I’m thinking about how things are going at home, but it’s not like I wake up and the whole day is just low from start to finish. It’s more situational, I guess. I can still function, and there are parts of the day where I’m okay or distracted enough that I’m not sitting in that feeling.\n\nOver the last couple weeks… maybe there have been moments, or parts of days, where my mood dips, but I haven’t really counted them, and I don’t think it’s been “most of the day” very often, if at all. I’d have a hard time giving you a number because I haven’t been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take as much pleasure or interest in the things you usually enjoy, even when you do manage to do them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… although I’d put it a little differently. It’s not that fishing or woodworking suddenly feel pointless if I actually get myself there. It’s more like I have a harder time getting myself to do them in the first place, and when I do, I’m not as settled into it as I used to be.\n\nThose things usually clear my head. Fishing especially — normally I can just be out there and everything kind of quiets down. Same with working on something in the garage, or going to those first-aid workshops. But since my father-in-law moved in, I feel like I’m holding myself together all day at work, and then when I’m home or trying to focus on coursework, I’m already wound up. So by the time I’d normally do something for myself, I’m short, tired, and kind of mentally somewhere else.\n\nSo yes, I guess I’ve noticed less interest and less enjoyment, but I don’t know that the activities themselves changed. It’s more like I’m not in the right headspace to get the same relief from them. And a lot of the time I just skip them altogether.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself getting irritated or angry more easily than usual, even over small things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the main reasons I came in, because it doesn’t feel like my usual baseline.\n\nAt work, I can mostly keep a lid on it because I have to. I’m an EMT, so when I’m on a call, there isn’t really room to be snappy or distracted — you just do the job. But once I’m home, or when I’m trying to sit down and do coursework, I’m already wound up, and I can hear myself being shorter than I mean to be.\n\nIt’s not like I’m exploding over everything, but small things get under my skin faster than they used to. I’ll answer in a sharper tone, or I’ll feel irritated before I’ve even really thought through what’s happening. And then afterward I’m thinking, why did I react like that? That’s not how I want to be.\n\nEver since my father-in-law moved in with us, it just feels like I’m carrying this tension around. And the things that usually help me burn that off or clear my head — fishing, woodworking, the first-aid workshops — I’ve been skipping those more and more. So I think the irritation kind of builds up because I’m not getting that normal reset.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have there been days when you felt down or experienced a loss of interest or pleasure for most of the day, even when there wasn’t an obvious reason for it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s been out of nowhere, if that’s what you mean. It feels pretty tied to what’s been going on at home.\n\nSince my father-in-law moved in with us, I feel like I’m kind of holding it together all day at work because I have to. Being an EMT, I can stay focused when I’m on duty — there’s structure, there’s a job to do, people are depending on me. But then when I get home, or when I try to sit down and do coursework, it’s like all that tension catches up with me. I’m more wound up and short than I’m used to being.\n\nAnd I have started skipping the things that normally help me keep my head clear — fishing, working on projects in the garage, even the first-aid workshops I usually like. So I wouldn’t say I’m down or uninterested for most of the day with no obvious reason. It’s more that there’s this ongoing stressor, and I’m not getting the usual reset from the things I used to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer take pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even when you’re not thinking about the situation at home?\n\nPatient: Not exactly in the way you’re asking, I don’t think. I wouldn’t say that I’m out fishing or in the garage and suddenly thinking, “I don’t enjoy this anymore,” completely separate from everything going on at home. It feels more connected than that.\n\nI came in because since my father-in-law moved in with us, I’ve been kind of holding myself together at work — and I can do that, because with EMT work you have to stay focused — but then at home, or when I’m trying to get through coursework, I’m more wound up and short than I’m used to. And because of that, I’ve started skipping the things that normally clear my head, like fishing, woodworking, and those first-aid workshops.\n\nSo I guess I still believe I enjoy those things, or at least I know I used to and I think I still would if I could get myself into the right frame of mind. But lately I’m just not getting to them as much, and when I do, I’m carrying that tension with me. It doesn’t feel like the enjoyment disappeared for no reason. It feels like the stress at home is bleeding into everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that even when things at home are calm, you still feel anxious or worried about other areas of your life, like work, school, or your health?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s broad worry in that way, no. I mean, work can be stressful because of what I do, and coursework takes focus, but I’m not sitting around worrying about everything — like my health, or random things going wrong, or work falling apart for no reason.\n\nIt feels much more tied to what’s happening at home. Ever since my father-in-law moved in with us, I feel like I’m using a lot of energy just keeping myself steady. At work I can do that because I have to — when you’re on a call, you just stay focused and handle what’s in front of you. But then when I get home, or when I try to sit down and get through schoolwork, I’m already wound up. I get short more easily than I used to, and it takes less to set me off internally.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is I’ve stopped doing the things that usually keep me balanced. I haven’t been fishing much, I’m not really doing woodworking in the garage, and I’ve been skipping the first-aid workshops I usually like. Those are usually the things that clear my head, so without them it just kind of builds. So even if the house is calm in the moment, I’m still carrying that tension from the whole situation, but I wouldn’t say I’m anxious across the board about every area of my life.\n\nDoctor: Even when things at home are calm, do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about other things like work, school, or your health, even if there isn’t an immediate problem?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense that I’m worrying about a bunch of separate things for no reason. I know work and school are part of my life, but the way this feels is still tied back to the situation at home.\n\nLike I said, since my father-in-law moved in with us, I can kind of keep myself together while I’m at work because I have to. When I’m on duty, there’s a call, there’s a task, you focus and do the job. But when I get home, or when I’m trying to sit down and deal with coursework, I notice I’m already keyed up. I’m more wound up, more short with people, just not as steady as I normally am.\n\nAnd I think part of why it keeps hanging around is that I’ve stopped doing the things that usually help me clear my head. I haven’t been getting out fishing, I’m not really spending time woodworking in the garage, and I’ve skipped the first-aid workshops I usually like. So even if things are calm in the house for a little while, I’m still carrying the stress from the whole setup. But I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there worried about my health or work or school out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Even when things at home are calm, do you find yourself worrying about other things like work, school, or your health more than usual?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s worry about a lot of other things in the way you mean, like my health or work just out of nowhere. It’s more that ever since my father-in-law moved in with us, I feel like I’m carrying this tension around.\n\nAt work, I can still hold it together because I have to — being an EMT, you don’t really get the option to fall apart or be distracted when there’s a job to do. But then when I’m home, or when I’m trying to sit down with coursework, I’m much more wound up than I’m used to. I get short more easily, and I can feel myself reacting harder to things than I normally would.\n\nAnd I think what’s made it worse is I’ve started dropping the things that usually help me stay even. I haven’t been fishing much, I’m not getting into the garage for woodworking, and I’ve been skipping the first-aid workshops I normally like. Those are the things that usually clear my head, so without them, the stress just kind of sits there. So, yes, I feel more on edge than usual, but it still feels connected to what’s going on at home more than worrying about everything else separately.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. But once I’m home, or when I’m trying to sit down and do coursework, I’m already wound up, and I can hear myself being shorter than I mean to be.\n\nIt’s not like I’m exploding over everything, but small things get under my skin faster than they used to. I’ll answer in a sharper tone, or I’ll feel irritated before I’ve even really thought through what’s happening. And then afterward I’m thinking, why did I react like that? That’s not how I want to be.\n\nEver since my father-in-law moved in with us, it just feels like I’m carrying this tension around. And the things that usually help me burn that off or clear my head — fishing, woodworking, the first-aid workshops — I’ve been skipping those more and more. So I think the irritation kind of builds up because I’m not getting that normal reset.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have there been days when you felt down or experienced a loss of interest or pleasure for most of the day, even when there wasn’t an obvious reason for it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s been out of nowhere, if that’s what you mean. It feels pretty tied to what’s been going on at home.\n\nSince my father-in-law moved in with us, I feel like I’m kind of holding it together all day at work because I have to. Being an EMT, I can stay focused when I’m on duty — there’s structure, there’s a job to do, people are depending on me. But then when I get home, or when I try to sit down and do coursework, it’s like all that tension catches up with me. I’m more wound up and short than I’m used to being.\n\nAnd I have started skipping the things that normally help me keep my head clear — fishing, working on projects in the garage, even the first-aid workshops I usually like. So I wouldn’t say I’m down or uninterested for most of the day with no obvious reason. It’s more that there’s this ongoing stressor, and I’m not getting the usual reset from the things I used to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer take pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even when you’re not thinking about the situation at home?\n\nPatient: Not exactly in the way you’re asking, I don’t think. I wouldn’t say that I’m out fishing or in the garage and suddenly thinking, “I don’t enjoy this anymore,” completely separate from everything going on at home. It feels more connected than that.\n\nI came in because since my father-in-law moved in with us, I’ve been kind of holding myself together at work — and I can do that, because with EMT work you have to stay focused — but then at home, or when I’m trying to get through coursework, I’m more wound up and short than I’m used to. And because of that, I’ve started skipping the things that normally clear my head, like fishing, woodworking, and those first-aid workshops.\n\nSo I guess I still believe I enjoy those things, or at least I know I used to and I think I still would if I could get myself into the right frame of mind. But lately I’m just not getting to them as much, and when I do, I’m carrying that tension with me. It doesn’t feel like the enjoyment disappeared for no reason. It feels like the stress at home is bleeding into everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that even when things at home are calm, you still feel anxious or worried about other areas of your life, like work, school, or your health?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s broad worry in that way, no. I mean, work can be stressful because of what I do, and coursework takes focus, but I’m not sitting around worrying about everything — like my health, or random things going wrong, or work falling apart for no reason.\n\nIt feels much more tied to what’s happening at home. Ever since my father-in-law moved in with us, I feel like I’m using a lot of energy just keeping myself steady. At work I can do that because I have to — when you’re on a call, you just stay focused and handle what’s in front of you. But then when I get home, or when I try to sit down and get through schoolwork, I’m already wound up. I get short more easily than I used to, and it takes less to set me off internally.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is I’ve stopped doing the things that usually keep me balanced. I haven’t been fishing much, I’m not really doing woodworking in the garage, and I’ve been skipping the first-aid workshops I usually like. Those are usually the things that clear my head, so without them it just kind of builds. So even if the house is calm in the moment, I’m still carrying that tension from the whole situation, but I wouldn’t say I’m anxious across the board about every area of my life.\n\nDoctor: Even when things at home are calm, do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about other things like work, school, or your health, even if there isn’t an immediate problem?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense that I’m worrying about a bunch of separate things for no reason. I know work and school are part of my life, but the way this feels is still tied back to the situation at home.\n\nLike I said, since my father-in-law moved in with us, I can kind of keep myself together while I’m at work because I have to. When I’m on duty, there’s a call, there’s a task, you focus and do the job. But when I get home, or when I’m trying to sit down and deal with coursework, I notice I’m already keyed up. I’m more wound up, more short with people, just not as steady as I normally am.\n\nAnd I think part of why it keeps hanging around is that I’ve stopped doing the things that usually help me clear my head. I haven’t been getting out fishing, I’m not really spending time woodworking in the garage, and I’ve skipped the first-aid workshops I usually like. So even if things are calm in the house for a little while, I’m still carrying the stress from the whole setup. But I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there worried about my health or work or school out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Even when things at home are calm, do you find yourself worrying about other things like work, school, or your health more than usual?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s worry about a lot of other things in the way you mean, like my health or work just out of nowhere. But then when I’m home, or when I’m trying to sit down with coursework, I’m much more wound up than I’m used to. I haven’t been fishing much, I’m not getting into the garage for woodworking, and I’ve been skipping the first-aid workshops I normally like. Those are the things that usually clear my head, so without them, the stress just kind of sits there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really like a general worry about everything, no. I mean, work and school are definitely harder to deal with when I’m already tense, but it doesn’t feel like I’m suddenly worried about my health or convinced something bad is going to happen at work for no reason.

It’s more that ever since my father-in-law moved in with us, I feel like I’ve been running at a higher level of tension all the time. At work I can still hold it together because I have to — as an EMT, when there’s a call or someone needs help, you focus on the task in front of you. But then when I get home, or when I’m trying to sit down and do coursework, I’m already wound up. I’m more short than I’m used to, and it takes less to irritate me.

And I’ve also let go of some of the things that usually keep me steady. I haven’t been fishing much, I’m not really doing woodworking, and I’ve skipped the first-aid workshops I normally enjoy. Those are usually how I clear my head, so without them, the stress just kind of stays with me. So I’d say I’m more on edge than I used to be, but it still feels tied to what’s going on at home.

My father-in-law moved in with us, and since then I’ve been kind of holding it together at work — I’m an EMT, so you sort of have to keep things contained there — but when I get home, or when I try to sit down for my coursework, I’m short, tense, just… not myself.\n\nThe thing that made me think maybe I should talk to someone is that I’ve started dropping the things that usually keep me level. I tell myself I’m too busy or too tired, but I think the truth is I’m just stressed and not handling the change at home as well as I expected to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a number of different things, like work, family, or health, and does it feel hard to control the worry?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “a number of different things,” exactly. I mean, when I’m tense, I do feel it in my body in the general sense — like I’m aware I’m keyed up — but I couldn’t honestly say, “yes, I’m shaking” or “yes, I’m sweating,” because I haven’t really noticed those specifically.\n\nSleep… I guess I’d have to think about that more. I don’t want to overstate it. I have nights where I don’t feel settled right away, but I’m not sure if that’s anything unusual or just me having a lot on my mind after the day. And muscle tightness — maybe? I mean, I’m an EMT, so I’m used to being physically tense during shifts, lifting, driving, being alert, all that. Sometimes it’s hard to separate what’s stress and what’s just the job or getting older.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: I haven’t noticed a big obvious physical thing like shaking or sweating. It’s more that I feel wound up, like my system doesn’t downshift as easily, but I haven’t paid enough attention to say much beyond that.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the situation with your father-in-law, do you ever feel restless or have trouble relaxing when there isn’t an obvious reason for it?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. I mean, I’m not saying I’m always perfectly relaxed when nothing’s going on — I’m an EMT, so being a little keyed up can kind of come with the territory — but what brought me in is that this feels tied pretty clearly to the change at home.\n\nSince my father-in-law moved in, it’s like I can keep the lid on during work because I have to, but then when I get home, or when I’m trying to sit down and focus on coursework, I’m already wound up. I get short faster than I normally would, and I have a harder time settling myself.\n\nAnd the thing that makes me think it’s not just random restlessness is that I’ve started dropping the stuff that usually helps me relax — fishing, woodworking, even those first-aid workshops I used to like going to. I’ll skip them and tell myself I’m busy or tired, but really I think I’m just not dealing with this home situation as well as I thought I was. So outside of that, no, I don’t think I’m just restless for no reason. It feels more connected to all of this.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even outside of the stress at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I feel hopeless exactly. That word feels a little bigger than what I’m experiencing. But I have noticed I’m not doing the things that normally help me feel like myself.\n\nLike, since my father-in-law moved in with us, I’ve been able to keep it together at work because I kind of have to — you can’t be distracted on calls, you know — but then when I get home, or when I’m trying to sit down and work on coursework, I’m already wound up. I’m more short with people than I’m used to being, and I don’t like that about myself.\n\nAnd the interest thing… yeah, that part’s probably fair. I’ve been skipping fishing, woodworking, even the first-aid workshops I used to enjoy going to. I’ll tell myself I’m just busy or tired, but if I’m being honest, those are usually the things that keep my head clear, and lately I just haven’t been making myself do them. So I don’t know if I’d call it being “down” across the board, but I’m definitely not getting the same relief from my usual routines because I’m not really doing them.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or in a low mood for most of the day, and if so, how often has that happened over the last couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve had days where I was down for most of the day. That doesn’t quite feel accurate to me.\n\nI’ve had stretches where I feel worn out or kind of discouraged, especially if I’m thinking about how things are going at home, but it’s not like I wake up and the whole day is just low from start to finish. It’s more situational, I guess. I can still function, and there are parts of the day where I’m okay or distracted enough that I’m not sitting in that feeling.\n\nOver the last couple weeks… maybe there have been moments, or parts of days, where my mood dips, but I haven’t really counted them, and I don’t think it’s been “most of the day” very often, if at all. I’d have a hard time giving you a number because I haven’t been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take as much pleasure or interest in the things you usually enjoy, even when you do manage to do them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… although I’d put it a little differently. It’s not that fishing or woodworking suddenly feel pointless if I actually get myself there. It’s more like I have a harder time getting myself to do them in the first place, and when I do, I’m not as settled into it as I used to be.\n\nThose things usually clear my head. Fishing especially — normally I can just be out there and everything kind of quiets down. Same with working on something in the garage, or going to those first-aid workshops. But since my father-in-law moved in, I feel like I’m holding myself together all day at work, and then when I’m home or trying to focus on coursework, I’m already wound up. So by the time I’d normally do something for myself, I’m short, tired, and kind of mentally somewhere else.\n\nSo yes, I guess I’ve noticed less interest and less enjoyment, but I don’t know that the activities themselves changed. It’s more like I’m not in the right headspace to get the same relief from them. And a lot of the time I just skip them altogether.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself getting irritated or angry more easily than usual, even over small things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the main reasons I came in, because it doesn’t feel like my usual baseline.\n\nAt work, I can mostly keep a lid on it because I have to. I’m an EMT, so when I’m on a call, there isn’t really room to be snappy or distracted — you just do the job. But once I’m home, or when I’m trying to sit down and do coursework, I’m already wound up, and I can hear myself being shorter than I mean to be.\n\nIt’s not like I’m exploding over everything, but small things get under my skin faster than they used to. I’ll answer in a sharper tone, or I’ll feel irritated before I’ve even really thought through what’s happening. And then afterward I’m thinking, why did I react like that? That’s not how I want to be.\n\nEver since my father-in-law moved in with us, it just feels like I’m carrying this tension around. And the things that usually help me burn that off or clear my head — fishing, woodworking, the first-aid workshops — I’ve been skipping those more and more. So I think the irritation kind of builds up because I’m not getting that normal reset.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have there been days when you felt down or experienced a loss of interest or pleasure for most of the day, even when there wasn’t an obvious reason for it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s been out of nowhere, if that’s what you mean. It feels pretty tied to what’s been going on at home.\n\nSince my father-in-law moved in with us, I feel like I’m kind of holding it together all day at work because I have to. Being an EMT, I can stay focused when I’m on duty — there’s structure, there’s a job to do, people are depending on me. But then when I get home, or when I try to sit down and do coursework, it’s like all that tension catches up with me. I’m more wound up and short than I’m used to being.\n\nAnd I have started skipping the things that normally help me keep my head clear — fishing, working on projects in the garage, even the first-aid workshops I usually like. So I wouldn’t say I’m down or uninterested for most of the day with no obvious reason. It’s more that there’s this ongoing stressor, and I’m not getting the usual reset from the things I used to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer take pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even when you’re not thinking about the situation at home?\n\nPatient: Not exactly in the way you’re asking, I don’t think. I wouldn’t say that I’m out fishing or in the garage and suddenly thinking, “I don’t enjoy this anymore,” completely separate from everything going on at home. It feels more connected than that.\n\nI came in because since my father-in-law moved in with us, I’ve been kind of holding myself together at work — and I can do that, because with EMT work you have to stay focused — but then at home, or when I’m trying to get through coursework, I’m more wound up and short than I’m used to. And because of that, I’ve started skipping the things that normally clear my head, like fishing, woodworking, and those first-aid workshops.\n\nSo I guess I still believe I enjoy those things, or at least I know I used to and I think I still would if I could get myself into the right frame of mind. But lately I’m just not getting to them as much, and when I do, I’m carrying that tension with me. It doesn’t feel like the enjoyment disappeared for no reason. It feels like the stress at home is bleeding into everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that even when things at home are calm, you still feel anxious or worried about other areas of your life, like work, school, or your health?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s broad worry in that way, no. I mean, work can be stressful because of what I do, and coursework takes focus, but I’m not sitting around worrying about everything — like my health, or random things going wrong, or work falling apart for no reason.\n\nIt feels much more tied to what’s happening at home. Ever since my father-in-law moved in with us, I feel like I’m using a lot of energy just keeping myself steady. At work I can do that because I have to — when you’re on a call, you just stay focused and handle what’s in front of you. But then when I get home, or when I try to sit down and get through schoolwork, I’m already wound up. I get short more easily than I used to, and it takes less to set me off internally.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is I’ve stopped doing the things that usually keep me balanced. I haven’t been fishing much, I’m not really doing woodworking in the garage, and I’ve been skipping the first-aid workshops I usually like. Those are usually the things that clear my head, so without them it just kind of builds. So even if the house is calm in the moment, I’m still carrying that tension from the whole situation, but I wouldn’t say I’m anxious across the board about every area of my life.\n\nDoctor: Even when things at home are calm, do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about other things like work, school, or your health, even if there isn’t an immediate problem?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense that I’m worrying about a bunch of separate things for no reason. I know work and school are part of my life, but the way this feels is still tied back to the situation at home.\n\nLike I said, since my father-in-law moved in with us, I can kind of keep myself together while I’m at work because I have to. When I’m on duty, there’s a call, there’s a task, you focus and do the job. But when I get home, or when I’m trying to sit down and deal with coursework, I notice I’m already keyed up. I’m more wound up, more short with people, just not as steady as I normally am.\n\nAnd I think part of why it keeps hanging around is that I’ve stopped doing the things that usually help me clear my head. I haven’t been getting out fishing, I’m not really spending time woodworking in the garage, and I’ve skipped the first-aid workshops I usually like. So even if things are calm in the house for a little while, I’m still carrying the stress from the whole setup. But I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there worried about my health or work or school out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Even when things at home are calm, do you find yourself worrying about other things like work, school, or your health more than usual?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s worry about a lot of other things in the way you mean, like my health or work just out of nowhere. It’s more that ever since my father-in-law moved in with us, I feel like I’m carrying this tension around.\n\nAt work, I can still hold it together because I have to — being an EMT, you don’t really get the option to fall apart or be distracted when there’s a job to do. But then when I’m home, or when I’m trying to sit down with coursework, I’m much more wound up than I’m used to. I get short more easily, and I can feel myself reacting harder to things than I normally would.\n\nAnd I think what’s made it worse is I’ve started dropping the things that usually help me stay even. I haven’t been fishing much, I’m not getting into the garage for woodworking, and I’ve been skipping the first-aid workshops I normally like. Those are the things that usually clear my head, so without them, the stress just kind of sits there. So, yes, I feel more on edge than usual, but it still feels connected to what’s going on at home more than worrying about everything else separately.\n\nDoctor: Even when things at home are going smoothly, do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about other things like work, school, or your health more than you used to?\n\nPatient: Not really like a general worry about everything, no. I mean, work and school are definitely harder to deal with when I’m already tense, but it doesn’t feel like I’m suddenly worried about my health or convinced something bad is going to happen at work for no reason.\n\nIt’s more that ever since my father-in-law moved in with us, I feel like I’ve been running at a higher level of tension all the time. At work I can still hold it together because I have to — as an EMT, when there’s a call or someone needs help, you focus on the task in front of you. But then when I get home, or when I’m trying to sit down and do coursework, I’m already wound up. I’m more short than I’m used to, and it takes less to irritate me.\n\nAnd I’ve also let go of some of the things that usually keep me steady. I haven’t been fishing much, I’m not really doing woodworking, and I’ve skipped the first-aid workshops I normally enjoy. Those are usually how I clear my head, so without them, the stress just kind of stays with me. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess the way I’d put it is, not out of nowhere, exactly. I don’t usually notice myself getting anxious when there’s truly nothing going on. It’s more like I’ve been carrying this stress from the whole situation at home, and even when the house is quiet for a bit, I’m still kind of wound up from it.

That’s really why I came in. Ever since my father-in-law moved in with us, I can keep it together at work because I have to — I’m used to focusing when I’m on a call or there’s a job in front of me. But once I’m home, or when I’m trying to sit down and work on coursework, I’m just more tense and short than I’m used to being. Like I don’t have the same patience or ability to let things roll off me.

And I think part of it is I’ve stopped doing the stuff that usually helps me reset. Fishing, woodworking in the garage, the first-aid workshops I normally like going to — I’ve been skipping those, and those are usually the things that clear my head. So I wouldn’t say it’s random anxiety with no stressor, but I do feel wound up even after the immediate thing has passed.

But then when I’m home, or when I’m trying to sit down with coursework, I’m much more wound up than I’m used to. I get short more easily, and I can feel myself reacting harder to things than I normally would.\n\nAnd I think what’s made it worse is I’ve started dropping the things that usually help me stay even. I haven’t been fishing much, I’m not getting into the garage for woodworking, and I’ve been skipping the first-aid workshops I normally like. Those are the things that usually clear my head, so without them, the stress just kind of sits there. So, yes, I feel more on edge than usual, but it still feels connected to what’s going on at home more than worrying about everything else separately.\n\nDoctor: Even when things at home are going smoothly, do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about other things like work, school, or your health more than you used to?\n\nPatient: Not really like a general worry about everything, no. I mean, work and school are definitely harder to deal with when I’m already tense, but it doesn’t feel like I’m suddenly worried about my health or convinced something bad is going to happen at work for no reason.\n\nIt’s more that ever since my father-in-law moved in with us, I feel like I’ve been running at a higher level of tension all the time. At work I can still hold it together because I have to — as an EMT, when there’s a call or someone needs help, you focus on the task in front of you. But then when I get home, or when I’m trying to sit down and do coursework, I’m already wound up. I’m more short than I’m used to, and it takes less to irritate me.\n\nAnd I’ve also let go of some of the things that usually keep me steady. I haven’t been fishing much, I’m not really doing woodworking, and I’ve skipped the first-aid workshops I normally enjoy. Those are usually how I clear my head, so without them, the stress just kind of stays with me. So I’d say I’m more on edge than I used to be, but it still feels tied to what’s going on at home.\n\nDoctor: Even when there are no obvious stressors or problems happening, do you ever feel anxious or notice physical symptoms like restlessness, fatigue, or muscle tension?\n\nPatient: I guess the way I’d put it is, not out of nowhere, exactly. I don’t usually notice myself getting anxious when there’s truly nothing going on. It’s more like I’ve been carrying this stress from the whole situation at home, and even when the house is quiet for a bit, I’m still kind of wound up from it.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. Ever since my father-in-law moved in with us, I can keep it together at work because I have to — I’m used to focusing when I’m on a call or there’s a job in front of me. But once I’m home, or when I’m trying to sit down and work on coursework, I’m just more tense and short than I’m used to being. Fishing, woodworking in the garage, the first-aid workshops I normally like going to — I’ve been skipping those, and those are usually the things that clear my head. So if something small happens, I react like I’m already at capacity. But I’m not sure it’s worry in the sense of, like, constant uncontrollable worrying about my health or money or every possible thing. It’s more tension, I guess, and having a hard time switching it off once I’m irritated or keyed up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical changes when you're feeling this tension, like trouble sleeping, shaking, sweating, or muscle tightness?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure I’ve been tracking it closely enough to give you a clean answer. I mean, when I’m tense, I do feel it in my body in the general sense — like I’m aware I’m keyed up — but I couldn’t honestly say, “yes, I’m shaking” or “yes, I’m sweating,” because I haven’t really noticed those specifically.\n\nSleep… I guess I’d have to think about that more. I don’t want to overstate it. I have nights where I don’t feel settled right away, but I’m not sure if that’s anything unusual or just me having a lot on my mind after the day. And muscle tightness — maybe? I mean, I’m an EMT, so I’m used to being physically tense during shifts, lifting, driving, being alert, all that. Sometimes it’s hard to separate what’s stress and what’s just the job or getting older.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: I haven’t noticed a big obvious physical thing like shaking or sweating. It’s more that I feel wound up, like my system doesn’t downshift as easily, but I haven’t paid enough attention to say much beyond that.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the situation with your father-in-law, do you ever feel restless or have trouble relaxing when there isn’t an obvious reason for it?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. I mean, I’m not saying I’m always perfectly relaxed when nothing’s going on — I’m an EMT, so being a little keyed up can kind of come with the territory — but what brought me in is that this feels tied pretty clearly to the change at home.\n\nSince my father-in-law moved in, it’s like I can keep the lid on during work because I have to, but then when I get home, or when I’m trying to sit down and focus on coursework, I’m already wound up. I get short faster than I normally would, and I have a harder time settling myself.\n\nAnd the thing that makes me think it’s not just random restlessness is that I’ve started dropping the stuff that usually helps me relax — fishing, woodworking, even those first-aid workshops I used to like going to. I’ll skip them and tell myself I’m busy or tired, but really I think I’m just not dealing with this home situation as well as I thought I was. So outside of that, no, I don’t think I’m just restless for no reason. It feels more connected to all of this.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even outside of the stress at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I feel hopeless exactly. That word feels a little bigger than what I’m experiencing. But I have noticed I’m not doing the things that normally help me feel like myself.\n\nLike, since my father-in-law moved in with us, I’ve been able to keep it together at work because I kind of have to — you can’t be distracted on calls, you know — but then when I get home, or when I’m trying to sit down and work on coursework, I’m already wound up. I’m more short with people than I’m used to being, and I don’t like that about myself.\n\nAnd the interest thing… yeah, that part’s probably fair. I’ve been skipping fishing, woodworking, even the first-aid workshops I used to enjoy going to. I’ll tell myself I’m just busy or tired, but if I’m being honest, those are usually the things that keep my head clear, and lately I just haven’t been making myself do them. So I don’t know if I’d call it being “down” across the board, but I’m definitely not getting the same relief from my usual routines because I’m not really doing them.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or in a low mood for most of the day, and if so, how often has that happened over the last couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve had days where I was down for most of the day. That doesn’t quite feel accurate to me.\n\nI’ve had stretches where I feel worn out or kind of discouraged, especially if I’m thinking about how things are going at home, but it’s not like I wake up and the whole day is just low from start to finish. It’s more situational, I guess. I can still function, and there are parts of the day where I’m okay or distracted enough that I’m not sitting in that feeling.\n\nOver the last couple weeks… maybe there have been moments, or parts of days, where my mood dips, but I haven’t really counted them, and I don’t think it’s been “most of the day” very often, if at all. I’d have a hard time giving you a number because I haven’t been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take as much pleasure or interest in the things you usually enjoy, even when you do manage to do them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… although I’d put it a little differently. It’s not that fishing or woodworking suddenly feel pointless if I actually get myself there. It’s more like I have a harder time getting myself to do them in the first place, and when I do, I’m not as settled into it as I used to be.\n\nThose things usually clear my head. Fishing especially — normally I can just be out there and everything kind of quiets down. Same with working on something in the garage, or going to those first-aid workshops. But since my father-in-law moved in, I feel like I’m holding myself together all day at work, and then when I’m home or trying to focus on coursework, I’m already wound up. So by the time I’d normally do something for myself, I’m short, tired, and kind of mentally somewhere else.\n\nSo yes, I guess I’ve noticed less interest and less enjoyment, but I don’t know that the activities themselves changed. It’s more like I’m not in the right headspace to get the same relief from them. And a lot of the time I just skip them altogether.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself getting irritated or angry more easily than usual, even over small things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the main reasons I came in, because it doesn’t feel like my usual baseline.\n\nAt work, I can mostly keep a lid on it because I have to. I’m an EMT, so when I’m on a call, there isn’t really room to be snappy or distracted — you just do the job. But once I’m home, or when I’m trying to sit down and do coursework, I’m already wound up, and I can hear myself being shorter than I mean to be.\n\nIt’s not like I’m exploding over everything, but small things get under my skin faster than they used to. I’ll answer in a sharper tone, or I’ll feel irritated before I’ve even really thought through what’s happening. And then afterward I’m thinking, why did I react like that? That’s not how I want to be.\n\nEver since my father-in-law moved in with us, it just feels like I’m carrying this tension around. And the things that usually help me burn that off or clear my head — fishing, woodworking, the first-aid workshops — I’ve been skipping those more and more. So I think the irritation kind of builds up because I’m not getting that normal reset.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have there been days when you felt down or experienced a loss of interest or pleasure for most of the day, even when there wasn’t an obvious reason for it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s been out of nowhere, if that’s what you mean. It feels pretty tied to what’s been going on at home.\n\nSince my father-in-law moved in with us, I feel like I’m kind of holding it together all day at work because I have to. Being an EMT, I can stay focused when I’m on duty — there’s structure, there’s a job to do, people are depending on me. But then when I get home, or when I try to sit down and do coursework, it’s like all that tension catches up with me. I’m more wound up and short than I’m used to being.\n\nAnd I have started skipping the things that normally help me keep my head clear — fishing, working on projects in the garage, even the first-aid workshops I usually like. So I wouldn’t say I’m down or uninterested for most of the day with no obvious reason. It’s more that there’s this ongoing stressor, and I’m not getting the usual reset from the things I used to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer take pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even when you’re not thinking about the situation at home?\n\nPatient: Not exactly in the way you’re asking, I don’t think. I wouldn’t say that I’m out fishing or in the garage and suddenly thinking, “I don’t enjoy this anymore,” completely separate from everything going on at home. It feels more connected than that.\n\nI came in because since my father-in-law moved in with us, I’ve been kind of holding myself together at work — and I can do that, because with EMT work you have to stay focused — but then at home, or when I’m trying to get through coursework, I’m more wound up and short than I’m used to. And because of that, I’ve started skipping the things that normally clear my head, like fishing, woodworking, and those first-aid workshops.\n\nSo I guess I still believe I enjoy those things, or at least I know I used to and I think I still would if I could get myself into the right frame of mind. But lately I’m just not getting to them as much, and when I do, I’m carrying that tension with me. It doesn’t feel like the enjoyment disappeared for no reason. It feels like the stress at home is bleeding into everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that even when things at home are calm, you still feel anxious or worried about other areas of your life, like work, school, or your health?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s broad worry in that way, no. I mean, work can be stressful because of what I do, and coursework takes focus, but I’m not sitting around worrying about everything — like my health, or random things going wrong, or work falling apart for no reason.\n\nIt feels much more tied to what’s happening at home. Ever since my father-in-law moved in with us, I feel like I’m using a lot of energy just keeping myself steady. At work I can do that because I have to — when you’re on a call, you just stay focused and handle what’s in front of you. But then when I get home, or when I try to sit down and get through schoolwork, I’m already wound up. I get short more easily than I used to, and it takes less to set me off internally.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is I’ve stopped doing the things that usually keep me balanced. I haven’t been fishing much, I’m not really doing woodworking in the garage, and I’ve been skipping the first-aid workshops I usually like. Those are usually the things that clear my head, so without them it just kind of builds. So even if the house is calm in the moment, I’m still carrying that tension from the whole situation, but I wouldn’t say I’m anxious across the board about every area of my life.\n\nDoctor: Even when things at home are calm, do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about other things like work, school, or your health, even if there isn’t an immediate problem?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense that I’m worrying about a bunch of separate things for no reason. When I’m on duty, there’s a call, there’s a task, you focus and do the job. But when I get home, or when I’m trying to sit down and deal with coursework, I notice I’m already keyed up. I’m more wound up, more short with people, just not as steady as I normally am.\n\nAnd I think part of why it keeps hanging around is that I’ve stopped doing the things that usually help me clear my head. I haven’t been getting out fishing, I’m not really spending time woodworking in the garage, and I’ve skipped the first-aid workshops I usually like. So even if things are calm in the house for a little while, I’m still carrying the stress from the whole setup. But I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there worried about my health or work or school out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Even when things at home are calm, do you find yourself worrying about other things like work, school, or your health more than usual?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s worry about a lot of other things in the way you mean, like my health or work just out of nowhere. It’s more that ever since my father-in-law moved in with us, I feel like I’m carrying this tension around.\n\nAt work, I can still hold it together because I have to — being an EMT, you don’t really get the option to fall apart or be distracted when there’s a job to do. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I’d describe it that way, no. I mean, I do worry, sure — I don’t want to make it sound like I’m completely relaxed or anything — but it’s not like my mind is jumping from my health to work to school to relationships and I can’t shut it off.

When I get worried, it usually feels connected to whatever is actually happening right in front of me, or something specific that’s already been going on. It’s not this broad, constant worry about a bunch of unrelated things. And if I’m busy or there’s a task to do, I can usually put it aside and focus.

So… no, I wouldn’t say I’m worrying about a number of different things for no clear reason, or that it feels uncontrollable in that way. It’s more situational, I guess.

That doesn’t quite feel accurate to me.\n\nI’ve had stretches where I feel worn out or kind of discouraged, especially if I’m thinking about how things are going at home, but it’s not like I wake up and the whole day is just low from start to finish. It’s more situational, I guess. I can still function, and there are parts of the day where I’m okay or distracted enough that I’m not sitting in that feeling.\n\nOver the last couple weeks… maybe there have been moments, or parts of days, where my mood dips, but I haven’t really counted them, and I don’t think it’s been “most of the day” very often, if at all. I’d have a hard time giving you a number because I haven’t been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take as much pleasure or interest in the things you usually enjoy, even when you do manage to do them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… although I’d put it a little differently. It’s not that fishing or woodworking suddenly feel pointless if I actually get myself there. It’s more like I have a harder time getting myself to do them in the first place, and when I do, I’m not as settled into it as I used to be.\n\nThose things usually clear my head. Fishing especially — normally I can just be out there and everything kind of quiets down. Same with working on something in the garage, or going to those first-aid workshops. But since my father-in-law moved in, I feel like I’m holding myself together all day at work, and then when I’m home or trying to focus on coursework, I’m already wound up. So by the time I’d normally do something for myself, I’m short, tired, and kind of mentally somewhere else.\n\nSo yes, I guess I’ve noticed less interest and less enjoyment, but I don’t know that the activities themselves changed. It’s more like I’m not in the right headspace to get the same relief from them. And a lot of the time I just skip them altogether.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself getting irritated or angry more easily than usual, even over small things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the main reasons I came in, because it doesn’t feel like my usual baseline.\n\nAt work, I can mostly keep a lid on it because I have to. I’m an EMT, so when I’m on a call, there isn’t really room to be snappy or distracted — you just do the job. But once I’m home, or when I’m trying to sit down and do coursework, I’m already wound up, and I can hear myself being shorter than I mean to be.\n\nIt’s not like I’m exploding over everything, but small things get under my skin faster than they used to. I’ll answer in a sharper tone, or I’ll feel irritated before I’ve even really thought through what’s happening. And then afterward I’m thinking, why did I react like that? That’s not how I want to be.\n\nEver since my father-in-law moved in with us, it just feels like I’m carrying this tension around. And the things that usually help me burn that off or clear my head — fishing, woodworking, the first-aid workshops — I’ve been skipping those more and more. So I think the irritation kind of builds up because I’m not getting that normal reset.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have there been days when you felt down or experienced a loss of interest or pleasure for most of the day, even when there wasn’t an obvious reason for it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s been out of nowhere, if that’s what you mean. It feels pretty tied to what’s been going on at home.\n\nSince my father-in-law moved in with us, I feel like I’m kind of holding it together all day at work because I have to. Being an EMT, I can stay focused when I’m on duty — there’s structure, there’s a job to do, people are depending on me. But then when I get home, or when I try to sit down and do coursework, it’s like all that tension catches up with me. I’m more wound up and short than I’m used to being.\n\nAnd I have started skipping the things that normally help me keep my head clear — fishing, working on projects in the garage, even the first-aid workshops I usually like. So I wouldn’t say I’m down or uninterested for most of the day with no obvious reason. It’s more that there’s this ongoing stressor, and I’m not getting the usual reset from the things I used to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer take pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even when you’re not thinking about the situation at home?\n\nPatient: Not exactly in the way you’re asking, I don’t think. I wouldn’t say that I’m out fishing or in the garage and suddenly thinking, “I don’t enjoy this anymore,” completely separate from everything going on at home. It feels more connected than that.\n\nI came in because since my father-in-law moved in with us, I’ve been kind of holding myself together at work — and I can do that, because with EMT work you have to stay focused — but then at home, or when I’m trying to get through coursework, I’m more wound up and short than I’m used to. And because of that, I’ve started skipping the things that normally clear my head, like fishing, woodworking, and those first-aid workshops.\n\nSo I guess I still believe I enjoy those things, or at least I know I used to and I think I still would if I could get myself into the right frame of mind. But lately I’m just not getting to them as much, and when I do, I’m carrying that tension with me. It doesn’t feel like the enjoyment disappeared for no reason. It feels like the stress at home is bleeding into everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that even when things at home are calm, you still feel anxious or worried about other areas of your life, like work, school, or your health?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s broad worry in that way, no. I mean, work can be stressful because of what I do, and coursework takes focus, but I’m not sitting around worrying about everything — like my health, or random things going wrong, or work falling apart for no reason.\n\nIt feels much more tied to what’s happening at home. Ever since my father-in-law moved in with us, I feel like I’m using a lot of energy just keeping myself steady. At work I can do that because I have to — when you’re on a call, you just stay focused and handle what’s in front of you. But then when I get home, or when I try to sit down and get through schoolwork, I’m already wound up. I get short more easily than I used to, and it takes less to set me off internally.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is I’ve stopped doing the things that usually keep me balanced. I haven’t been fishing much, I’m not really doing woodworking in the garage, and I’ve been skipping the first-aid workshops I usually like. Those are usually the things that clear my head, so without them it just kind of builds. So even if the house is calm in the moment, I’m still carrying that tension from the whole situation, but I wouldn’t say I’m anxious across the board about every area of my life.\n\nDoctor: Even when things at home are calm, do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about other things like work, school, or your health, even if there isn’t an immediate problem?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense that I’m worrying about a bunch of separate things for no reason. I know work and school are part of my life, but the way this feels is still tied back to the situation at home.\n\nLike I said, since my father-in-law moved in with us, I can kind of keep myself together while I’m at work because I have to. When I’m on duty, there’s a call, there’s a task, you focus and do the job. But when I get home, or when I’m trying to sit down and deal with coursework, I notice I’m already keyed up. I’m more wound up, more short with people, just not as steady as I normally am.\n\nAnd I think part of why it keeps hanging around is that I’ve stopped doing the things that usually help me clear my head. I haven’t been getting out fishing, I’m not really spending time woodworking in the garage, and I’ve skipped the first-aid workshops I usually like. So even if things are calm in the house for a little while, I’m still carrying the stress from the whole setup. But I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there worried about my health or work or school out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Even when things at home are calm, do you find yourself worrying about other things like work, school, or your health more than usual?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s worry about a lot of other things in the way you mean, like my health or work just out of nowhere. It’s more that ever since my father-in-law moved in with us, I feel like I’m carrying this tension around.\n\nAt work, I can still hold it together because I have to — being an EMT, you don’t really get the option to fall apart or be distracted when there’s a job to do. But then when I’m home, or when I’m trying to sit down with coursework, I’m much more wound up than I’m used to. I get short more easily, and I can feel myself reacting harder to things than I normally would.\n\nAnd I think what’s made it worse is I’ve started dropping the things that usually help me stay even. I haven’t been fishing much, I’m not getting into the garage for woodworking, and I’ve been skipping the first-aid workshops I normally like. Right, yeah — I know I’m kind of repeating myself, but that’s the part that feels important to me. I wouldn’t say it comes from nowhere, exactly. It’s not like I’m sitting there with everything fine and suddenly I’m anxious for no reason.

It’s more that ever since my father-in-law moved in with us, I’ve been carrying this tension around. My father-in-law moved in with us, and since then I’ve been kind of holding it together at work — I’m an EMT, so you sort of have to keep things contained there — but when I get home, or when I try to sit down for my coursework, I’m short, tense, just… not myself.\n\nThe thing that made me think maybe I should talk to someone is that I’ve started dropping the things that usually keep me level. I fish, I do woodworking, and I used to go to first-aid workshops, partly because I enjoy them and partly because they clear my head. Lately I’ve been skipping them. I tell myself I’m too busy or too tired, but I think the truth is I’m just stressed and not handling the change at home as well as I expected to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a number of different things, like work, family, or health, and does it feel hard to control the worry?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “a number of different things,” exactly. I mean, I do think about work, and family, and whether I’m doing enough, sure — but it doesn’t feel like I’m lying awake making a whole list of disasters, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more like… there’s this one main pressure point at home, and then everything else kind of gets filtered through that. So if something small happens, I react like I’m already at capacity. But I’m not sure it’s worry in the sense of, like, constant uncontrollable worrying about my health or money or every possible thing. It’s more tension, I guess, and having a hard time switching it off once I’m irritated or keyed up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical changes when you're feeling this tension, like trouble sleeping, shaking, sweating, or muscle tightness?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure I’ve been tracking it closely enough to give you a clean answer. I mean, when I’m tense, I do feel it in my body in the general sense — like I’m aware I’m keyed up — but I couldn’t honestly say, “yes, I’m shaking” or “yes, I’m sweating,” because I haven’t really noticed those specifically.\n\nSleep… I guess I’d have to think about that more. I don’t want to overstate it. I have nights where I don’t feel settled right away, but I’m not sure if that’s anything unusual or just me having a lot on my mind after the day. And muscle tightness — maybe? I mean, I’m an EMT, so I’m used to being physically tense during shifts, lifting, driving, being alert, all that. Sometimes it’s hard to separate what’s stress and what’s just the job or getting older.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: I haven’t noticed a big obvious physical thing like shaking or sweating. It’s more that I feel wound up, like my system doesn’t downshift as easily, but I haven’t paid enough attention to say much beyond that.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the situation with your father-in-law, do you ever feel restless or have trouble relaxing when there isn’t an obvious reason for it?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. I mean, I’m not saying I’m always perfectly relaxed when nothing’s going on — I’m an EMT, so being a little keyed up can kind of come with the territory — but what brought me in is that this feels tied pretty clearly to the change at home.\n\nSince my father-in-law moved in, it’s like I can keep the lid on during work because I have to, but then when I get home, or when I’m trying to sit down and focus on coursework, I’m already wound up. I get short faster than I normally would, and I have a harder time settling myself.\n\nAnd the thing that makes me think it’s not just random restlessness is that I’ve started dropping the stuff that usually helps me relax — fishing, woodworking, even those first-aid workshops I used to like going to. I’ll skip them and tell myself I’m busy or tired, but really I think I’m just not dealing with this home situation as well as I thought I was. So outside of that, no, I don’t think I’m just restless for no reason. It feels more connected to all of this.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even outside of the stress at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I feel hopeless exactly. That word feels a little bigger than what I’m experiencing. But I have noticed I’m not doing the things that normally help me feel like myself.\n\nLike, since my father-in-law moved in with us, I’ve been able to keep it together at work because I kind of have to — you can’t be distracted on calls, you know — but then when I get home, or when I’m trying to sit down and work on coursework, I’m already wound up. I’m more short with people than I’m used to being, and I don’t like that about myself.\n\nAnd the interest thing… yeah, that part’s probably fair. I’ve been skipping fishing, woodworking, even the first-aid workshops I used to enjoy going to. I’ll tell myself I’m just busy or tired, but if I’m being honest, those are usually the things that keep my head clear, and lately I just haven’t been making myself do them. So I don’t know if I’d call it being “down” across the board, but I’m definitely not getting the same relief from my usual routines because I’m not really doing them.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or in a low mood for most of the day, and if so, how often has that happened over the last couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve had days where I was down for most of the day. That doesn’t quite feel accurate to me.\n\nI’ve had stretches where I feel worn out or kind of discouraged, especially if I’m thinking about how things are going at home, but it’s not like I wake up and the whole day is just low from start to finish. It’s more situational, I guess. I can still function, and there are parts of the day where I’m okay or distracted enough that I’m not sitting in that feeling.\n\nOver the last couple weeks… maybe there have been moments, or parts of days, where my mood dips, but I haven’t really counted them, and I don’t think it’s been “most of the day” very often, if at all. I’d have a hard time giving you a number because I haven’t been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take as much pleasure or interest in the things you usually enjoy, even when you do manage to do them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… although I’d put it a little differently. It’s not that fishing or woodworking suddenly feel pointless if I actually get myself there. It’s more like I have a harder time getting myself to do them in the first place, and when I do, I’m not as settled into it as I used to be.\n\nThose things usually clear my head. Fishing especially — normally I can just be out there and everything kind of quiets down. Same with working on something in the garage, or going to those first-aid workshops. But since my father-in-law moved in, I feel like I’m holding myself together all day at work, and then when I’m home or trying to focus on coursework, I’m already wound up. So by the time I’d normally do something for myself, I’m short, tired, and kind of mentally somewhere else.\n\nSo yes, I guess I’ve noticed less interest and less enjoyment, but I don’t know that the activities themselves changed. It’s more like I’m not in the right headspace to get the same relief from them. And a lot of the time I just skip them altogether.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself getting irritated or angry more easily than usual, even over small things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the main reasons I came in, because it doesn’t feel like my usual baseline.\n\nAt work, I can mostly keep a lid on it because I have to. I’m an EMT, so when I’m on a call, there isn’t really room to be snappy or distracted — you just do the job. But once I’m home, or when I’m trying to sit down and do coursework, I’m already wound up, and I can hear myself being shorter than I mean to be.\n\nIt’s not like I’m exploding over everything, but small things get under my skin faster than they used to. I’ll answer in a sharper tone, or I’ll feel irritated before I’ve even really thought through what’s happening. And then afterward I’m thinking, why did I react like that? That’s not how I want to be.\n\nEver since my father-in-law moved in with us, it just feels like I’m carrying this tension around. And the things that usually help me burn that off or clear my head — fishing, woodworking, the first-aid workshops — I’ve been skipping those more and more. So I think the irritation kind of builds up because I’m not getting that normal reset.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have there been days when you felt down or experienced a loss of interest or pleasure for most of the day, even when there wasn’t an obvious reason for it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s been out of nowhere, if that’s what you mean. It feels pretty tied to what’s been going on at home.\n\nSince my father-in-law moved in with us, I feel like I’m kind of holding it together all day at work because I have to. Being an EMT, I can stay focused when I’m on duty — there’s structure, there’s a job to do, people are depending on me. But then when I get home, or when I try to sit down and do coursework, it’s like all that tension catches up with me. I’m more wound up and short than I’m used to being.\n\nAnd I have started skipping the things that normally help me keep my head clear — fishing, working on projects in the garage, even the first-aid workshops I usually like. So I wouldn’t say I’m down or uninterested for most of the day with no obvious reason. It’s more that there’s this ongoing stressor, and I’m not getting the usual reset from the things I used to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer take pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even when you’re not thinking about the situation at home?\n\nPatient: Not exactly in the way you’re asking, I don’t think. It feels more connected than that.\n\nI came in because since my father-in-law moved in with us, I’ve been kind of holding myself together at work — and I can do that, because with EMT work you have to stay focused — but then at home, or when I’m trying to get through coursework, I’m more wound up and short than I’m used to. And because of that, I’ve started skipping the things that normally clear my head, like fishing, woodworking, and those first-aid workshops.\n\nSo I guess I still believe I enjoy those things, or at least I know I used to and I think I still would if I could get myself into the right frame of mind. It feels like the stress at home is bleeding into everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that even when things at home are calm, you still feel anxious or worried about other areas of your life, like work, school, or your health?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s broad worry in that way, no. I mean, work can be stressful because of what I do, and coursework takes focus, but I’m not sitting around worrying about everything — like my health, or random things going wrong, or work falling apart for no reason.\n\nIt feels much more tied to what’s happening at home. Ever since my father-in-law moved in with us, I feel like I’m using a lot of energy just keeping myself steady. At work I can do that because I have to — when you’re on a call, you just stay focused and handle what’s in front of you. But then when I get home, or when I try to sit down and get through schoolwork, I’m already wound up. I get short more easily than I used to, and it takes less to set me off internally.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is I’ve stopped doing the things that usually keep me balanced.
